Post: Somebody please mansplain why DIDs are better than URLs?

You still need a resolver, and you can own your own domain name, so I don't see how a DID is functionally any different from a DNS based identity.

You're just moving the ownership of your identity from ICANN to some other system.

#atproto
Replies count: 1
    Reply: My main peeve is the claim that DIDs are "portable".

Man, fuckin URLs are portable too! Move your website to a new domain and the relative links will still work. Only the domain has to change, which is exactly the case if you move your DID to another resolver.

Post: In evm, you can delegate rights to someone else (like a smart-contract) using permissions or other methods. This is not possible in ATP.

Having the keys on the server means that users don't actually own their data, because the PDS operator owns it. Why is this a good design for a social network?
Replies count: 1
    Reply: Most people don't want key custody, that's why most crypto users use exchanges anyway. There should be more competing PDS operators but that will happen over time. In the meantime atproto is custodial for all who want it. Like me!
Post: Just like AT Protocol lets users:
- use any client

- keep identity across network

The EVM advantage is similar:
- use any wallet
- choose any layer
- move assets between layers

Post: Extrapolating from the blog post, there are two sets of secrets from the privileged environment that were used in the exploit and wouldn't have been available to pull_request:

- A CacheServerUrl and AccessToken, to poison the cache.
- A PyPI API key, enabling persistence after the vuln was removed.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: There’s no confirmation yet but the attacker may have also exfiltrated a GitHub PAT, since the workflow has a `_GITHUB_TOKEN` custom secret (note the underscore)
Post: hear me out: reality tv show where we vote one person off atproto every week
Replies count: 0
Post: The privileged env can contain secrets like API keys. pull_request is very limited:

> With the exception of GITHUB_TOKEN, secrets are not passed to the runner when a workflow is triggered from a forked repository. The GITHUB_TOKEN has read-only permissions in pull requests from forked repositories.
Replies count: 2
    Reply: Extrapolating from the blog post, there are two sets of secrets from the privileged environment that were used in the exploit and wouldn't have been available to pull_request:

- A CacheServerUrl and AccessToken, to poison the cache.
- A PyPI API key, enabling persistence after the vuln was removed.
    Reply: Yep, exactly this — pull_request is “unprivileged” relative to pull_request_target because forks don’t have access to the secrets context or default GITHUB_TOKEN. So the attacker needed that trigger, not a weaker one like pull_request

Post: My #Swiftlang #AProto API library, ATProtoKit, has been updated to 0.21.0.

The way you authenticate has been tweaked a little. This will help with implementing OAuth but managing the session should hopefully be a little easier.

More tweaks will be on the way, but this is the first step.

    Reply: You can learn more about this change in the documentation: atprotokit.cjrriley.com/documentatio...

Post: account migration has been in the protocol for a while! I have a blog post and CLI tool ("goat") which makes it pretty smooth.

migrating followers "just works" in that follow relationships are keyed on account identity (DID), which doesn't change during migration. unlike AP don't need to update.
Replies count: 2
    Reply: that’s not migration though, that’s just changing your handle

migration would be moving from a did:plc to a did:web or other such case. as far as i can tell, this is *not* in the protocol — you can change handle or change storage but these are not the same as migrating to a new account
    Reply: in terms of adding account migration to app or UI, what we might do is put more account mgmt features like that in a PDS web UI. there is a hint of that with OAuth flow (you do client authorization in PDS web UI).

putting it there means each app doesn't need to re-impl (and add to security surface)

Post: Ok yes, Relays currently expose getRepo per docs.bsky.app/docs/api/com.... There's some discussion here github.com/bluesky-soci..., too. (And the new non-archival Relay Rainbow is just an implementation detail of scaling bsky.network, nice.)
Replies count: 3
    Reply: Since blocks have no ordering we can do backfill by getting them from two sources in parallel: subscribeRepos (the firehose) and getRepos (backfill).

We just need to be able to file blocks concurrently under the same repository.

To reduce duplication, we can prioritize getRepos for missing repos.
    Reply: The currently prod relay doesn't support getRepo at the moment, (we had to switch to a non-archival relay temporarily and are still running on it while working to get our Archival relay back online). So you'll have to talk to the canonical PDS for the repo to call getRepo (this scales better anyhow)
    Reply: I was under the impression you could go crawl the list of repos and call get repo on each?
Post: atproto posts are already signed so that step is taken care of. 

In other words, you can be confident that a post is signed by the handle you see that created it.

What users need help with is quickly identifying that "alice.reuters.com" a Reuters account not "alice.rueters.com" or something funky.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: great! BUT, here's where i think this approach is still applicable to the problem of impersonation/verification:

bob creates /bob.bsky.social. bob then generates a unique key pair. his public key is, lets say: XYZ42. this is bobs key and bobs key alone, its unique to him and him alone.
Post: Ideally, we'd keep only the commit, records, and their path names. The Merkle Search Tree can be rebuilt.

However, I don't think there is an efficient way to know what records have been deleted without rebuilding the graph (see atproto.com/specs/reposi...) and I'd like to be almost write-only.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: Next best thing is to update each repo's latest commit from the diffs that come from the firehose, and store all other blocks.

The commit has the hash of the MST head node block, which transitively has the hash of every other content-addressed block. We can clean up on read, which is rarer.

Post: Hypothetically, if I was building something like an AppView 🙃 would it make more sense to have foreign keys for user IDs as integers everywhere, with a user table mapping int to DID, or to have DID strings everywhere and do joins using strings? Would such string joins be slower? #mysql #postgres
Replies count: 11
    Reply: I went through the same dillema in millipds, I do ID integers. I can declare them as foreign keys which enforces a certain amount of consistency (afaik it means I can't delete a user and leave their "orphaned" records hanging around?)
    Reply: 
“using a GUID or hash as a primary key can be slow because it requires more storage space and can lead to slower insert performance as the tree has to be rebalanced”

From https://planetscale.com/learn/courses/mysql-for-developers/indexes/primary-keys?autoplay=1
    Reply: Here's a hypothetical good luck. Hypothetically, how would you size your queries? I mean the purist in me says mapping table. The design isn't that complex and the architecture scales. But if this is anything under xl, xxl [banks, major online shopping sites] just do DID for simplicity.
    Reply: I'd assume that joins and lookups by 32-char strings would be slower than by ints, but maybe not (given that with a separate table mapping to ints I'd need to always do an extra lookup there)?… 🤔
    Reply: www.sqlite.org/np1queryprob...
    Reply: I’d go with an UUIDv7 foreign key:
• more flexibility in case there is ever a DID change, a DID conflict or you want to support multiple DIDs per user
• takes less space on all the tables that reference the user
• v7 grows monotonically over time, so no index rebalancing needed
    Reply: dids - just make sure you always index on did

Depending on the data model, I’d be tempted to store dids as pk once in some table (maybe your handle mapping table) and use it as a foreign key everywhere
    Reply: Imho hypothetical indexing and joins on integers would many times faster than on long strings, especially if you use multiple tables, and even more if you could "hide" the DID in a table you use anyway so you don't need a separate mapping table.
    Reply: Consider DID as UUID and have a look here -> www.baeldung.com/uuid-vs-sequ...
    Reply: We currently use DIDs everywhere. No user table which I would describe as more architecturally pure - we don't own the user entity within the AppView. @will.frontpage.team is obsessed with adding a user table for this purpose though 😂
    Reply: UUIDs are very popular as ids in databases (and longer than DID). Use DID.

Post: In the context of this post, "Blacksky" is a few different things:
- A custom feed which aggregates content (likes, posts, etc.) to display a custom timeline (e.g. Trending)
- A moderation service that labels posts and accounts as anti-black or hidden
- A server where users' account data is hosted

	reverse_proxy pds:3000
}

Post: A PDS is your atproto repository in the cloud. It stores your posts/likes/follows/etc and acts as a proxy to the network for your client.

For technical people, it's very low effort to run your own PDS. You rent a ~$5/mo server and run the handy installer script.

Not at all necessary but it's cool!

Post: #atproto #atdev 
Is there any reason bsky.social fails when calling resolveHandle, if the user is self hosting? See for example:

 curl -G --data-urlencode "handle=sergio.bsky.col.social" bsky.social/xrpc/com.atp...

> {"error":"InvalidRequest","message":"Unable to resolve handle"}
Replies count: 1
    Reply: bsky.social is an entryway, it doesn't implement com.atproto.identity.resolveHandle. Make your query against public.api.bsky.app.

Additionally, GET requests against xrpc endpoints use query string parameters, not urlencoded bodies.

    Reply: /subscribe
    Reply: effectively zero effort

I've moved this PDS between multiple servers as I was shaking things out (first Rocky Linux, then Ubuntu then back to Debian). Turn off the PDS service, rsync everything over, make sure DNS points to the new one, turn it on and you're golden.

Post: technical question: on #atproto, what makes blocks necessary to be public but not block list subscriptions?
Replies count: 1
    Reply: i think the actual answer lies somewhere between:
- public everything was likely a dorseyist thing
- lists came months after blocks, and private list subscriptions violate dorseyism
- public blocks can be used as network optimization
- maybe there are some practical differences between blocks, but..
Post: SMTP is a really really bad example. It's theoretically decentralized, but it practically leads to oligopolies in mass scale.

The goal of the AT Protocol should not be to try to replicate 40 year old protocols, but to come up with one that is relevant today.
Replies count: 2
    Reply: I think the main problem is the PDSs are federated to the central AppView & there's no mobile p2p lightclient checking for censorship
    Reply: Gotcha. It should be like more modern protocols like Bitcoin (where 3-4 mining orgs have most of the hash power) or  Ethereum (where a similar number controls more than half the stake)... all while having no built-in token incentives and providing a smooth migration path. 👍

Post: Okay #atproto and #bluedev folk; from what I gathered it's not possible for an app to use oauth to sign in if it's not a web app, am I correct there? Or am I wrong? Haelp...
Replies count: 2
    Reply: Normally, an Oauth exchange involves three parties - the service, a client and a client back end. Doesn't have to be web, but your app does need a private server to keep secure.

There are client-only flows, but they are less secure and usually restricted to special use cases.
    Reply: Hey dude, if you look at atproto.com section about OAuth there is a part about app types or something similar and they have an option for native apps which might be what you are looking for.

Post: I have a newbie #atproto #atdev q: I've read atproto.com/specs/did & github.com/did-method-p... but just want to confirm I understand correctly: a user's did will NEVER change, correct? ie. even if there's a key rotation/identity mutation later, the *original hash* will still be a user's did?

TIA!🙏
Replies count: 2
    Reply: correct. it is a stable identifier, as any did-method has to be
    Reply: Correct; the hash part's based on the genesis operation (the one that "creates" the DID document I guess) and as such will never change; the mutable part is the 'alsoKnownAs' bit where handles and such live. It's like DNS, really. IP always stays the same but you can change the name pointing to it.

Post: - arrays of strings are a smell. you usually want an array of objects, even with a single field each, so you can extend later
- agonize over record schemas; they are content-addressed and distributed. API endpoints are more flexible
Replies count: 1
    Reply: I've been on this app for about a day and I'm already learning new things.
Post: - in API responses ("views") with a record, usually want to include the full AT-URI and CID of that record as well
- group metadata as sub-objects. eg, "viewer" context should probably all be a single object (we didn't do this for all bsky lexicons)
Replies count: 1
    Reply: - arrays of strings are a smell. you usually want an array of objects, even with a single field each, so you can extend later

Post: it's idiomatic to the protocol - and I do have trust that bluesky doesn't *want* to have to employ a bunch of verification people

Replies count: 1
    Reply: Yeah, nobody sensible wants to run a verification service if they don't have to! But that's in serious tension with "a lot of people are really gullible and information-illiterate", sigh
Post: - a "defs" Lexicon is an emerged pattern for stuff at NSID group which might be re-used. but doing other defs/fragments per file is also allowed/ok
- try to keep records a single-word noun, and try to have API endpoints not be single-word; eg verbNoun
Replies count: 1
    Reply: - have bytes string limits be roughly 10x grapheme cluster limits. use graphemes as real limits for real text and names; bytes-only good for identifiers (eg, DOI or ISBN)
- if you need floats, you can put them in a string for now
- use string formats when possible. eg, use 'uri' for any URL fields
Post: - open unions are great for evolution / extensibility. recommend against "closed" unions unless really sure Forever
- leave space for arrays of labels in API responses
- think about empty response bodies, and leave door open to adding a body. prop need longer guidance on this
Replies count: 1
    Reply: - for now, don't use periods in field names, so NSIDs can be jammed in there as an extensibility thing
- make field names compatible with variables/symbols in programming languages, for easy codegen
- the norm that has emerged is camelCase, for better or worse
Post: - return the full record in "view" API endpoints. we didn't do this very consistently in current bsky lexicons
- feel free to re-use the rich text "facets" from bsky. happy to chat about ripping that out to non-app.bsky namespace if helpful? but hope code/parsing can be shared
Replies count: 2
    Reply: the hashtag and mention facets should probably stay in app.bsky but the link one can probably be genericized to com.atproto. bold, italics, underline, and inline code, if and when they exist, should probably also be under com.atproto since they're so generic and potentially common
    Reply: - open unions are great for evolution / extensibility. recommend against "closed" unions unless really sure Forever

Post: The atproto network is designed to be cheap to crawl, which is why PDS hosts have an exposed and permissionless sync API.

One of the major problems with the web is how inaccessible web sites are to anyone that isn't Google.

Being open has its cons but I think they're far out-weighed by the pros.
Replies count: 2
    Reply: This was my major response to the HF drama.
It isn’t about “security” or “permissions” it’s about consent.
    Reply: That’s a really good point. It’s so much easier to crawl since instead of arbitrary file directories it’s all structured data behind an API

Crawlers are just clients
Post: Sure, just an example of a good "permissionless" service at scale.

Public atproto repos publish data that is supposed to be easily scraped, fetched, and indexed.

If Relays don't offer good service then devs will hit PDS hosts.

And if both are locked down the goal of an open network will suffer.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: Forcing bad actors and people looking to extract value from the data to either

1. run expensive crawling infrastructure
2. Pay for access

Have been the existing models that have proven to work long term

Post: We could build HTTP4 based off the SSH2 if we really wanted to.

It would be a very bad idea, and none of the SSH or HTTP protocol people would like it, but it's *technically* possible.

There's a lot of information on the web about ActivityPub vs atproto if you're interested.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: Quite a few overlay networks based on SSH and mainly serving http. It's not HTTP4, just a different L4. And there is a proposal for SSH over h2/h3 (using TLS). SSH over h2 or wss is old news.
Post: Why isn't HTTP compatible with Gopher?

Why isn't SSH compatible with Telnet?

Why isn't SFTP compatible with FTP?

Why isn't IMAP compatible with POP3?

Why isn't JSON compatible with XML?

Why isn't ActivityPub compatible with NNTP?

Creating new protocols solve technical AND cultural problems.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: In all of these examples, there was compatibility. Servers offered the new protocols in addition without abandoning the old ones.

Of course, new protocols are essential. But that doesn't mean burning bridges to existing protocols & applications.

For me, that's a central philosophy of the Internet.

Post: Each lookup is for a different record

TXT _atproto.alice.example.com "did:..."

TXT _atproto.sam.example.com "did:..."

There are also DNS servers that support database lookups, so it can be done much the same way the HTTP option can be. But this is less common.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: Oh, I got it now! Each subdomain will only have a single TXT record so you can still pretty efficiently `nslookup -type=TXT alice.example.com`

Post: When building an #atprotocol app, think in these 4 keys step:
1. Design app specific data schema 
2. Persistent storage for app specific records
3. Application logic for Create/Update/Delete user's data
4. Read from firehose to aggregate data updates across the network.

Post: tl;dr DPoP lets the auth server bind sessions to possession of some keypair. If the client stores the keypair securely (e.g. non-extractable WebCrypto keys), then a compromised auth token (e.g. leaked via XSS, passive SSL MitM) does not compromise the session as a whole.

Post: 作り直してPDS動いた。

PDSのデータはそのまま(VMを入れ替えるとかで)別サーバーへ「移行」したい場合

(1) 旧環境の/pdsをバックアップ
(2) 新環境のVM作成
(3) DNSのレコード設定を新環境のIPアドレスに行くように設定
(4) 新環境のファイルシステムに旧環境の/pdsをリストア
(5) 新環境へ通常の手順でPDS構築(デプロイしたPDSはリストアしたデータをマウントする

これでOKだった

https://github.com/bluesky-social/pds/blob/main/README.md
Replies count: 1
    Reply: 旧環境はpdsサービス停止して、新環境はdocker logs -f pdsでログが流れてるので期待通り動いてるはず。

Post: I think there's a big misunderstanding about AT Protocol and Bluesky that I've fallen into

We're building AT Protocol as a decentralized platform, but I always thought the goal was *also* to make Bluesky itself decentralized. To have one microblogging app that works for everyone.
Replies count: 2
    Reply: I think you have to think “protocol”

Like email is a (very successful) protocol, you have many apps to connect - you can self host or use Gmail, your identity and content is portable and composable. 

We need this for micro-blogging too. AT, ActivityPub, Farcaster, Lens are all  trying to built it.
    Reply: It is now clear that this is not the goal, and the main way to make the microblogging use case decentralized and censorship-resistant is to make more and more apps. To me, this misses the point a bit.

Post: This is the kind of direction I've been thinking in. Use of plc.directory as an agreed-upon central server for *resolution* can and should be separated from the current dependency on plc.directory for *operation validity* (writes/inclusion needing trust, and canonical ordering via its global clock).

Post: exactly. the goal of the sync protocol (including firehose) is to be able to build complete, perfect copies of the MST repo structure. it is hard to prove you are doing that correctly looking at individual diffs.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: realistically, many consumers of the firehose don't care about this! which is fine. but it is helpful for everybody if a relay can do this "complete" verification, so others don't need to bother
Post: we have jetstream already.

in the long term, sure, efficiency would be great. for now, we are still fairly focused on delivering on core guarantees, like ensuring it is *possible* to do full synchronization. that is what enables "exit", which is a core value of atproto
Replies count: 3
    Reply: Jetstream is great and a step in the right direction, but unfortunately the filters are only for first party, not for subject events
i.e. if I wanted to know who mentioned me, or who followed me I would have to listen to all events then filter on my end
    Reply: Makes sense. Long term I would love to be able to define a subsection of the network and its dependent posts, and subscribe to that feed only, retaining authentication checks.
    Reply: and jetstream has been far superior, takes way less bandwidth, and very few have moved to it.

more should move to it. Skyfeeds is working right now to move to it. I'm on it. It's really great. 

There's no need for the firehose for feeds.

Post: much like your drivers license has your address written on it (and can be re-issued if you move), your atproto identity is tied to a "DID document", which describes where your data is hosted on the atproto/bluesky network.

(DID = Decentralised Identity)
Replies count: 1
    Reply: your DID document can also be updated, and that's a big part of where the "billionaire resistance" comes from - if Bluesky PBC turns bad, you can seamlessly migrate your whole account to another provider, by updating your DID document.

Post: I want to know what is #atproto. And like how is this app is working or like concept can anybody help please 🙏.
Replies count: 2
    Reply: Super simplified version:

Each user is a PDS. A PDS is a blob of data that sends things to Relays. Relays are servers that store a bunch of things that PDSs send to them. 

Then, an Appview comes and takes those things and formats them into tweets/posts/skeets.

Post: ...but yes, ATProto is not flat p2p. Just like in the fediverse you have to get your mentions from your instance, in ATProto you (currently) have to get your mentions from an appview or the firehose.

    Reply: But in the Fediverse the one who makes a mention will send it to your instance so your instance doesn’t need to sift through a full firehose

Post: IMO doing a separate websocket for this would be pretty heavy? but that is just opinion.

other things considered have been an arg on createRecord to PDS, which has the PDS hold the HTTP request open until it polls and confirms an indicated service (by DID) has processed the rev
Replies count: 3
    Reply: having a standardized long-poll endpoint on the appview would be cool!
    Reply: Sure? Swap out a global connection to an application-local stream of events that is filtered by a singular application-local relay connection. Either way, having the notification that everything was processed and having the assurance that your event was propagated is the goal.
    Reply: really glad to see these questions/issues getting raised! when we hit these issues, we took note that we'd know when dev ecosystem was maturing when others started hitting exactly this. we are committed to making sure there are solid devex/UX solutions to this problem for all
Post: there is an existing system for this, though it isn't well indicated in specs/docs.

basically can do *any* auth'd request to an appview, and get back an HTTP header (Atproto-Repo-Rev) indicating the most recent repo rev processed for the account making the request
Replies count: 1
    Reply: this was originally added for PDS to intercept and help manage consistency between multiple client devices, or read-after-write situations. that use-case is half-baked b/c we want PDS to be fully app-agnostic. so need a generic proto-level way to do this.

Post: When consuming from jetstream, does that count as network ingress or egress for hosting providers?

    Reply: Pretty sure everyone counts bytes in/out regardless of the connection direction

Post: AT Protocol works in such a way that each repository (user account) has its own independent merkle-tree and relay is the place where it is aggregated. So it works a little differently than you think.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: I see. Thanks for the clarification. So why wouldn’t you be able to replicate just a subset of the network then, if each user has their own merkle-tree? Perhaps there are parts of the state which are global (like followers), but you should be able to index only what you’re interested in.
Post: You can't index a subset if you don't know what the subset is.

simple example - Followers count - in order to get the number of followers, you have to index *all* PDS. Thats how current AT Protocol architecture works.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: AT Protocol works in such a way that each repository (user account) has its own independent merkle-tree and relay is the place where it is aggregated. So it works a little differently than you think.

Post: Every decentralized network must offer some incentives to attract participants. If it's not token and financial incentives, then often the incentive is the very fact that the network is decentralized. AT Protocol offers neither. There's no reason to build on it.
Replies count: 4
    Reply: bsky.app/profile/wang...
    Reply: keep money out of posting honestly
    Reply: I think a nice example of this phenomenon is Nostr. It has far fewer users, but because it's effectively decentralized, a lot of people build on it. I'd say much more than people build on ATP.

nostrapps.com
    Reply: Devs are incentivized to build where the users are. ATproto is not fully decentralized yet, but the protocol is open so its the best bet currently in the market. Nostr is too technical to reach mainstream adoption in its current form.

Post: The system we have built with ATProto (the underlying tech of bluesky) is an extremely successful (comparatively speaking) manifestation of the underlying ethos of the decentralization space. We took the best learnings from the past decade of work there and made a social network with them

    Reply: We use private keys and merkle trees to provide user authority over their data, we have open apis and open participation in our network, we allow users the right to participate with us entirely on their own terms and on their own hardware and/or software

Post: Make sure you're resuming sessions and using the refreshToken rather than logging in fresh each time. When you create a session you get an accessToken which is valid for 30-90 minutes and then a refreshToken which is valid for 2 months and can be used to get a new access and refresh token.

Post: Yup, there are almost fifteen million SQLite databases on Bluesky’s PDS servers. It’s wildly efficient and simple but not without trade offs of course.

Makes sense for this use case in large part because each users atproto repository is self contained, with links to other repos, like a website.

    Reply: Oh I should add that the data is aggregated on a separate service (the AppView) for the app to consume in a friendly and high performance way.

    Reply: That’s also convenient for db-inclusive unit testing 😍
Post: Sure, but there are win-win scenarios and the protocol and network was designed to avoid this being a problem, by being “locked open” in case the company that launched it ever became evil. Doesn’t mean it’s not a valid concern though!

Post: 1. (not on the team anymore, so won't answer for them)

2. SQLite is used on the PDS, stored on the local (NVMe SSD). Each (of us) users has our own SQLite database for our atproto repository data.

Here's the original PR where @dholms.xyz and @divy.zone did this work: github.com/bluesky-soci...
Replies count: 0
Post: The sqlites are one-per-user on the write path and there isn't really much single-user concurrency when it comes to writes so we haven't seen any issues there at all. The PDSs themselves have a shared sequencer SQLite but it's in WAL mode and the writes are small, each PDS only does <50 write/sec.

Post: I wasn't satisfied with existing Python libraries for working with DAG-CBOR, so of course I wrote my own: github.com/DavidBuchana...
Replies count: 1
    Reply: With deterministic serialisation, combined with the Merkle part of the MST, the entire repo state can be boiled down to a single hash (the MST root), which can easily be cryptographically signed as part of a "commit" object atproto.com/specs/reposi...
Post: DAG-CBOR is a *deterministic* binary serialisation format with a JSON-like data model.

DAG-CBOR spec: ipld.io/docs/codecs/...

atproto docs on their specific "profile" of DAG-CBOR and associated data model: atproto.com/specs/data-m...
Replies count: 1
    Reply: I wasn't satisfied with existing Python libraries for working with DAG-CBOR, so of course I wrote my own: github.com/DavidBuchana...
Post: The A in atproto stands for authenticated, and here, authentication means cryptographic signing. atproto records are "JSON", but signing JSON is hard (I wrote more about that here www.da.vidbuchanan.co.uk/blog/signing...)

atproto solves this by encoding records (and MST nodes) in DAG-CBOR format
Replies count: 2
    Reply: DAG-CBOR is a *deterministic* binary serialisation format with a JSON-like data model.

DAG-CBOR spec: ipld.io/docs/codecs/...

atproto docs on their specific "profile" of DAG-CBOR and associated data model: atproto.com/specs/data-m...
    Reply: Blog post gives me flashbacks to Apple's cursed signing of plists in MobileAsset.

    Reply: With simplicity in mind, the entire application/repo state is persisted inside a single sqlite db - including blobs! (this may be subject to change, if it causes issues)

Post: Question for #atprotocol folks. When it comes to sensitive data like DM's, is that stored in a centralized db versus in the atmosphere? I'm trying to wrap my head around how to handle sensitive data
Replies count: 2
    Reply: DMs are currently a centralized service that was kinda rushed because users demanded it. there are plans to expand atproto into supporting various kinds of private data, including e2e encryption for things such as DMs
    Reply: Right now everything related directly to atproto is public; the DMs work as a feature inside the AppView. IIRC they were implemented in a rush because the users were demanding it and it was the start of the user waves caused by Musk, and that was the safest method.

Post: We use OTEL for tracing in most of our services going to a local Tempo instance, we log using svlogd writing to a local logging directory that gets tailed by promtail and shipped to a local Loki instance. Grafana agents on each host get host metrics and scrape local containers to forward to Victoria
Replies count: 1
    Reply: Some of our services ship _all_ traces to a monitoring host and that monitoring host runs a Grafana Agent that does tail sampling for everything. Allows us to avoid spending CPU on the application hosts for trace sampling logic when we've got enough local bandwidth that it doesn't hurt.
Post: hrm! that Linode number is quite a bit more expensive than the couple hundred/month that both Alice and I found.

current relay impl stores most data as files-on-disk; postgresql is an index

    Reply: yeah my current estimate is a €200/mo hetzner dedi for a full relay
    Reply: we don't see every network participant needing to store a full mirror of the entire network, any more than every web browser needs a full search index of the entire web.

the goal is to have enough infra diversity to have alternatives and credible exit.
Post: Our compute density requirements differ pretty heavily from what oxide provides, we lean pretty heavily on one or more machines for specific services with very little admin overhead (no k8s, no docker NAT) and we use AMD Genoa CPUs with up to 192c384t per system. Also prices don't make sense for us.
Replies count: 1
    Reply: The oxide racks make more sense for Hyperscalars but we don't really have those kinds of workloads. We basically run a big, dense, all-NVME DB cluster and then some high compute and high BW services with varying CPU to RAM ratios and some local disk on single-purpose machines.

Post: Their entire infra runs out of RAM and has no persistence. This diskless infra was audited in 2023 by Radically Open Security. No information leakage or logging of customer data was found. Several other issues were discovered and subsequently addressed by Mullvad - mullvad.net/en/blog/infr...
Replies count: 1
    Reply: For situations in which a VPN is determined to be the best solution, Mullvad is pretty solid.

Remember that a VPN is not a silver bullet for privacy and things like your browser window size, installed fonts, GPU quirks, and more can be used to fingerprint and track you whether or not you use a VPN.

Replies count: 0
Post: the CDM is responsible for generating the license request, which is sent off to the licensing server by the browser, which returns a license response (slight simplification)

the CDM uses the response to derive the required key material, using whitebox crypto and/or hardware security features
Replies count: 1
    Reply: the derived key material - usually symmetric "content keys" - is held within the CDM, and is used to decrypt the media using, again, either whitebox crypto or hardware security features
